Regression Adjustments for Double Randomization in Two-Sided Marketplaces
Timothy Sudijono, Lihua Lei, Lorenzo Masoero, Suhas Vijaykumar, Guido Imbens, James McQueen

Abstract
Multiple randomization designs (MRDs) are a class of experimental designs used to handle interference in two-sided marketplaces. We investigate regression adjustment strategies for estimating total, spillover, and direct effects in MRDs. We derive minimum asymptotic variance estimators among a broad class of linearly adjusted estimators, without assuming a linear model on the potential outcomes. Surprisingly, the optimal regression adjustments are estimable from data and are generally different from regression adjustments in classical randomized experiments. For example, one such optimal estimator for the direct effect corresponds to a weighted regression with interacted two-way fixed effects. We establish model-robustness properties, central limit theorems, and inferential methods for our estimators, relying on improved theoretical results for MRD experiments. Our results provide the analog of classical regression adjustments for marketplace experiments. Numerical simulations demonstrate a considerable increase in efficiency over simpler approaches, enabling better inference when running MRDs.



\declaretheorem

[name=Theorem,numberwithin=section]thm

Keywords:
Covariate Adjustment, Randomization Inference, Design-based Inference, Spillovers, Marketplaces

1
Introduction

Two-sided marketplaces are ubiquitous in the digital economy. In these settings, standard experimental designs (A/B tests) aimed at measuring the impact of an intervention can fall short due to
interference
: interventions can lead to strategic interactions (or “spillovers”) within or across groups. Multiple Randomization Designs (MRDs) are a rich class of experimental designs
[
1
,
16
]
designed to handle interference in two-sided marketplaces. In MRDs, the outcome of interest is indexed by a buyer-seller pair and treatments can be implemented at this level. Under a
local interference
assumption,
[
1
,
22
]
use MRDs to produce estimands and corresponding estimators that capture cross-unit interference and obtain corresponding estimates of spillover effects. A missing piece in the existing MRD framework is how covariate adjustments can be leveraged to enhance the precision of the corresponding estimators, similarly to what is done for typical A/B tests
[
7
]
. Real-world MRDs can suffer from low power due to the explicit randomization over both buyers and sellers in the experiment, especially if one side of the marketplace is small. By incorporating covariates such as the outcome
Y_{ij}
from a previous time period,
we hope to enable more powerful inference for these experiments. In this work we fill the gap and provide regression adjustment methodologies for MRDs, resolving a significant barrier to using MRDs in practice.

1.1
Background

Fix a two-sided marketplace with
I
buyers and
J
sellers. For each buyer-seller pair
(i,j)
we observe an outcome
y_{ij}
of interest. Further, associated to each buyer-seller pair
(i,j)
is a vector of covariates
X_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which may represent past observed outcomes, or individual buyer / seller level characteristics.

We will consider
simple multiple randomization designs
(SMRDs). The SMRD assigns variables
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}\in\{0,1\}
to each buyer, as in a completely randomized experiment, with
\sum_{i=1}^{I}W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=I_{T}
, the total number of treated buyers. Similarly each seller is assigned a binary variable
W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}\in\{0,1\}
, with
J_{T}
total treated sellers. All buyer-seller pairs
(i,j)
for which
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=1
are given a binary treatment
W_{ij}=\text{T}
, while all other pairs serve as control (if
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=0
, then
W_{ij}=\text{C})
. An example of such a treatment matrix
\mathbf{W}=(W_{ij})_{ij}
is given as follows. Here, we consider a marketplace of
4
buyers and
8
sellers, where the first two buyers and first four sellers are given treatment:

\mathbf{W}=\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{T}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\text{C}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{C}}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(1)

The
local interference
assumption of
[
1
]
is used to identify several estimands of interest.

Definition 1.1

(Local Interference Assumption)
.

Potential outcomes satisfy the
local interference
assumption if
y_{ij}(\mathbf{W})=y_{ij}(\mathbf{W}^{\prime})
for any pair
(i,j)
such that 1)
W_{ij}=W^{\prime}_{ij}
, 2) the fraction of treated sellers for buyer
i
are the same for
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
, and 3) the fraction of treated buyers for seller
j
are the same for
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.

Under the local interference assumption, each buyer-seller pair has four potential outcomes indexed by
\gamma\in\Gamma:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\}
, defined as follows:

\gamma_{ij}=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}&\text{if }W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=1,W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=1\\
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}&\text{if }W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=1,W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}&\text{if }W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=0,W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=1\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}&\text{if }W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=0,W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=0.\end{cases}

The
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}
group consists of directly treated pairs, while the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}
group serves as a clean, homogeneous control in which both the buyer and seller are unexposed to treatment. The
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}
groups are control pairs (
W_{ij}=\text{C}
) whose buyer (resp. seller) is assigned treatment and therefore interacts with treated units on the other side of the marketplace; these three control groups (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}
) are comparable prior to treatment, yet can differ post-treatment due to spillovers. Their comparison is what enables SMRDs to detect and measure interference.
Similar forms of interference were previously considered in
[
21
]
(cf. “anonymous interactions”) and in
[
14
]
(cf. “stratified interference”).
For a detailed example of a two-sided marketplace in which local interference is at play, refer to
[
23
, Example 2.5]

In a nutshell, in that example the authors present a two-sided platform model where content creators and advertisers interact. Each creator-advertiser pair generates revenue based on their respective quality scores and a compatibility factor. The platform compensates both parties through revenue-sharing contracts, while creators and advertisers incur costs to maintain quality. The model includes a binary intervention in the form of additional incentives that affects revenue generation.
The key insight is that agents’ strategic responses to these incentives create local interference: the outcome of any creator-advertiser interaction depends not only on their direct treatment status but also on the average treatment status of all their other interactions. This leads to equilibrium outcomes (both revenue and profit) that exhibit local interference structure, making it a natural setting to study spillover effects in two-sided marketplaces.

Estimands.

We henceforth use the notation

\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}:=\frac{1}{IJ}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}y_{ij}(\gamma),\gamma\in\Gamma

to denote the average of the potential outcomes for a given type
\gamma
. We are primarily interested in the causal estimands

\tau_{c}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma},

for any vector
c=[c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}]
. Taking
c=[1,0,0,-1],[1,-1,-1,1]
recover the
total effect
and
direct effect
respectively, while
c=[0,1,0,-1],[0,0,1,-1]
measure
buyer
and
seller spillover effects
defined in
[
1
,
23
]
. The buyer (and seller) spillover captures the effect of having a treated buyer (resp. seller) on a given interaction. Intuitively, the direct effect captures the average impact on a pair
(i,j)
solely from the intervention. The total effect may be thought of as the unit-level average effect of the randomized treatment policy on the whole marketplace. It may be decomposed into the direct effect of the treatment plus the effect of buyer and seller spillovers, under the local interference assumption
[
23
]
.

1.2
Motivation: the direct effect

To showcase our results and the importance of thinking carefully about regression adjustment, consider the direct effect estimator proposed in
[
23
]
:

\hat{\tau}_{\dir}:=\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}y_{ij}-\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}y_{ij}-\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}y_{ij}+\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}J_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}y_{ij}.

(2)

It is not difficult to see that the direct effect estimator can be written as the coefficient
\tau
in the regression

y_{ij}\sim\mu+\alpha W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}+\delta W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+\tau W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}.

(3)

One simple way of incorporating covariates into this regression is to consider the ordinary least squares (OLS)

y_{ij}\sim\mu+\alpha W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}+\delta W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+\tau W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+X_{ij}^{\top}\beta,

(4)

which can be seen as an analog of Fisher’s ANCOVA discussed in
[
10
,
8
]
or the analog of CUPED from A/B Testing
[
7
]
.
The estimated coefficient
\hat{\tau}
serves as a regression-adjusted estimate of the direct effect. The method serves as a good baseline; under mild assumptions,
\hat{\tau}
is consistent for the direct effect.

However, when the regression specification (
4
) does not correspond to the true model for
y_{ij}
, there exists an estimator which is provably more efficient than ANCOVA, in large samples. The intuition is the following. Let
e_{ij}=y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta
and define

\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\beta):=\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}e_{ij}-\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}e_{ij}-\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}J_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}e_{ij}+\frac{1}{I_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}J_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}\sum_{(i,j)\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}e_{ij}.

(5)

By inspecting the first order condition of the OLS,
\tau
in (
4
) is equal to
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\hat{\beta})
for
\hat{\beta}
minimizing the OLS condition. The form of (
5
) also suggests a more efficient estimator, by choosing
\beta
to minimize the variance of (
5
). A priori, it seems like
\beta
depends on unobservable potential outcomes, but there exists a consistent estimator
\hat{\beta}
of the optimal adjustment. This insight is implicit in
[
18
,
20
]
in the cross-sectional case and it is also closely related to control variates from Monte Carlo. We elucidate the former connection further in
Section

1.4
. This suggests the simple plug-in
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\hat{\beta})
.

Surprisingly, the plug-in estimator for the direct effect is interpretable and equal to the coefficient
\tau
in the
weighted
least squares

\displaystyle y_{ij}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{ij}}}{{\sim}}(\mu+\mu^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\mu^{\textsf{S}}_{j})+(\alpha+\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}+(\delta+\delta^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\delta^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+(\tau+\tau^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\tau^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+X_{ij}^{\top}\beta

(6)

\displaystyle w_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}^{2}J_{\gamma}^{2}},(i,j)\in\gamma,

under suitable identifiability constraints. This is a weighted difference-in-differences panel regression with interacted two-way fixed effects. Importantly, we do not assume the regression specification (
6
) gives the correct model. In fact, this weighted least squares estimator has the minimal asymptotic variance among the class (
5
), without any structural assumptions on the outcome distribution conditional on covariates.

The weights are closely related to the tyranny-of-minority regression
[
20
]
, which minimizes the asymptotic variance with the same coefficient
\beta
shared across all groups in cross-sectional experiments.
Empirically, using the optimal regression adjustment
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\hat{\beta})
can significantly improve estimation and inference.

Figure

1
shows a situation in which naive regression adjustment according to ANCOVA (
4
) can actually hurt asymptotic variance compared to no adjustment at all. Similar results were demonstrated by Freedman
[
10
]
in the cross-sectional case, for imbalanced trials. At the same time, the figure demonstrates that the optimal adjustment can be much more precise than naive adjustment. Further details on this simulation are discussed in
Section

4
, with inferential results discussed therein.

Figure 1
:
KDE estimate of sampling distributions for the direct effect estimators i) without adjustment, ii) with the ANCOVA adjustment, and iii) with the plug-in optimal adjustment.

1.3
Contributions

In this work, we primarily analyze regression adjustments of the form

\hat{\tau}_{c}(\beta):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left(y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta\right),

(7)

where
I_{\gamma},J_{\gamma}
denote the number of buyers and sellers respectively in a block
\gamma
(cf. (
1
)). This is equal to
I_{T},J_{T}
or
I_{C},J_{C}
depending on
\gamma.
We will refer to these as
non-interacted imputation estimators
. These imputation estimators generalize both the unadjusted estimators and naive methods of regression adjustment. Our contributions are the following.

1.

Optimal Regression Adjustments.
For general effects of interest, we derive the choice of
\beta
which minimizes the variances of the imputation estimators, absent any structural assumptions on the potential outcomes. In all cases, the optimal choices of
\beta
can be estimated from observed data; they do not depend on unidentifiable contrasts of potential outcomes. This leads to natural plug-in estimators which provably outperform the unadjusted estimators in terms of asymptotic variance. Furthermore, the optimal adjustments are generally different from ordinary least squares.

2.

Theory and Methods for Inference.
We show asymptotic normality of the regression adjustment strategy and accompanying conservative confidence intervals for the regression adjusted estimator.

3.

Empirical Performance.
In several simulation settings, we demonstrate efficiency gain of the optimal regression adjustment over other methods. The accompanying confidence intervals are also shorter while maintaining desired coverage.

4.

New Theoretical Tools for MRDs.
Our theoretical results for the plug-in estimators rely on improvements to the theory of doubly-randomized sums, which gives inference for vanilla MRDs. This is another key contribution of this paper. In particular, we give a new CLT for doubly-randomized sums in
1
-Wasserstein distance which shows asymptotic normality in settings where Theorem 4.6 of
[
23
]
does not apply. The CLT uses a novel approach based on Stein’s method and concentration of measure tools for sampling without replacement. Moreover, we show the asymptotic consistency of the conservative variance of
[
23
]
under a similar suite of assumptions to our CLT.

The results are proved in a purely finite population framework where the potential outcomes are treated as fixed and the only randomness comes from the explicit randomization of
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
. Previous work in the finite population framework
[
19
,
18
,
17
,
20
]
typically assumes a sequence of finite populations of potential outcomes in which various quantities such as variances and population regression adjustments converge to limits. For example, consider the difference-in-means estimator for a finite population
\{Y_{i}(1),Y_{i}(0)\}_{i=1}^{n}
, when sampling
n_{0}
units without replacement. Its variance is well known and given by

\frac{1}{n_{0}}S_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}S_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}S_{\tau}^{2}

(8)

with
n_{1}=n-n_{0},

S_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}(i)-\bar{Y}(i))^{2}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, and similarly
S_{\tau}^{2}
denotes the finite-population variance of the individual treatment effects. By way of a super-population setup and the law of large numbers, the quantities
S_{0}^{2},S_{1}^{2},S_{\tau}^{2}
are presumed to have non-zero constant limits. Therefore, the variance of the difference-in-means estimator scales like
\Theta(1/n)
.

The analogous situation in the MRD setting is less straightforward, comprising one challenge in this work. The variance of the direct effect estimator (
2
) is more complicated and is recorded in (
26
). Roughly the formula is of the form

\frac{1}{I}\omega^{\textsf{B}}+\frac{1}{J}\omega^{\textsf{S}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\omega^{\textsf{BS}},

(9)

where
\omega^{\textsf{B}},\omega^{\textsf{S}},\omega^{\textsf{BS}}
are linear combinations of finite population variance quantities. Assuming that these quantities have constant limits raises a problem here, as then
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{\dir})
can either scale like
\Theta(I^{-1}+J^{-1})
,
\Theta((IJ)^{-1})
, or in-between as
\Theta(I^{-\alpha}),\alpha\in[1,2].
Example
C.1
details one such situation. This challenge has not been highlighted before in previous work;
[
23
]
primarily requires that
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c})=\Omega(I^{-1}+J^{-1})
for their results to hold.

A comprehensive analysis of MRDs in all possible scaling regimes is a significant research program; we take the first step in this direction. For our inferential results on the plug-in regression adjustment procedure
\hat{\tau}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})
, we make use of the previous assumption that
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c})=\Theta(I^{-1}+J^{-1})
and in some important examples, show how we can go beyond this. These inferential results rely on improved theoretical tools for MRDs without covariates which apply to the regime where
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c})=\Theta((IJ)^{-1})
. We detail theoretical questions for future research in
Section

5
and note some avenues for improvement. Regardless, our novel estimators have clear practical utility and our analysis covers a suite of interesting examples.

Another natural class to consider is the class of
interacted
imputation estimators:

\hat{\tau}_{c,\text{int}}(\bm{\beta}):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left(y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta_{\gamma}\right)

(10)

where a separate
\beta_{\gamma}
is used for each group and
c=[c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}]=[1,-1,-1,1].
These strictly nest the estimators of (
7
). The same insight is true here: one can estimate the
\beta_{\gamma}
’s which minimize the
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\bm{\beta}))
and define a plug-in estimator. As the discussion of the interacted estimators requires more complicated notation, we will only discuss this class further in
Appendix

F
. Our techniques for the non-interacted case extend naturally to the interacted case. Moreover, the non-interacted estimators (
5
) are worth studying in their own right, especially if one of the four MRD groups is small relative to the number of covariates. In such cases, (
10
) may be unstable compared to (
7
).
[
20
]
details several practical and theoretical benefits of non-interacted versus interacted adjustments in cross-sectional experiments.

1.4
Related Work

Online Experimentation.

Online randomized experiments (A/B tests) have become a standard tool to drive innovation in the technology industry
[
4
,
25
]
.
In the data analysis phase, experimenters often perform regression adjustment with pre-experimental covariates. This yields estimates that can be significantly more reliable than those obtained without adjustment
[
22
]
.

Standard practice in online experimentation is to expose a completely random subset of units to treatment. While extremely useful, these standard experimental designs fall short when interventions (i) differentially impact multiple populations of units (e.g., an intervention on an online service that influences interactions between buyers and sellers) and/or (ii) lead to strategic behavior that affects the measured outcomes within or across these groups (e.g., the treatment causes buyers and sellers to adjust their behavior in a manner that influences their other, untreated interactions). Acknowledging the novel challenges posed by online experiments and the inability of standard experiments to address them, a number of novel estimators and experimental designs have been proposed over the last few years.
In particular, a number of authors have focused on proposing experimental designs that are robust to settings in which there is
interference
– of the sort outlined above – across units in the experiment
[
2
,
26
,
27
,
4
]
.

Multiple Randomization Designs.

Among these proposals, Multiple Randomization Designs (MRDs) have emerged as a flexible and rich class of designs
[
16
,
2
]
for two-sided marketplaces of buyers and sellers, where the outcome of interest is indexed by a buyer-seller pair.
[
16
]
motivates the design as a way to reduce bias, under a Markov Decision Process model.
[
1
]
adopt the finite-population potential outcome framework to show how — under a general notion of interference across units, referred to as
local interference
— it is possible to use MRDs to produce estimands and corresponding “unadjusted” estimators to capture cross-unit interference and obtain corresponding estimates of spillover effects.
Exact variances and corresponding variance estimators are also provided.
While flexible, a missing piece and open question in the existing MRD framework is how covariate adjustments may be leveraged in order to enhance the precision of the corresponding estimators, similarly to what is done for “standard” experiments
[
7
,
19
]
.

Beyond the standard buyer-seller marketplace, MRDs have found applications in a variety of experimental settings. When the second dimension corresponds to time, the design reduces to a
switchback
(or crossover) experiment, where units alternate between treatment and control across time periods; such designs have been studied and deployed at scale in industry
[
5
,
24
]
. MRDs can also be combined with cluster randomization, where one side of the marketplace is randomized at the level of groups of units rather than individuals
[
15
]
.

Regression Adjustments in Cross-Sectional Experiments.

For standard randomized control trials with two treatment groups, regression adjustment has a long history. Most relevant to this work are
[
10
,
19
,
18
,
20
]
. To explain the story and how it relates to our results, a little notation is helpful. Define
Y_{i}(0),Y_{i}(1),i=1,\dots,n
potential outcomes,
Z_{i}
the treatment indicator, and
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
associated covariates.
Z_{i}
are indicators of sampling without replacement such that
\sum_{i}Z_{i}=n_{1}.

The seminal work
[
19
]
addresses previous complaints of regression adjustment by Freedman
[
10
,
9
]
. Freedman argued that regression adjustments of the form
Y_{i}\sim Z_{i}+X_{i}
can hurt asymptotic variance compared to regular difference-in-means
Y_{i}\sim Z_{i}
especially when the treatment and control groups are imbalanced. In
[
19
]
, it is argued that using the interacted regression
Y_{i}\sim X_{i}+Z_{i}+X_{i}Z_{i}
addresses these concerns. Lin’s interacted regression solution is particularly satisfying. Firstly, it is model-robust, not requiring the linearity specification to hold. Secondly, it always improves on the asymptotic variance of both difference-in-means and naive regression adjustment. Thirdly, conservative inference is easily done using robust standard errors in the interacted regression. Moreover, it is shown that a certain weighted regression, the
tyranny-of-minority
(ToM) regression, also has the same asymptotic properties as Lin’s interacted regression. These results are all justified in a finite population framework, where the potential outcomes are treated as fixed and the only randomness is from the randomization
Z_{i}
.

The results of
[
19
]
appear mystical; fortunately, several works
[
18
,
8
,
12
]
elucidate the interacted regression. The idea is to consider the imputation estimator

\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{1})-\frac{1}{n-n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-Z_{i})(Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{0})

(11)

akin to (
10
). When the covariates are centered, the form of (
11
) suggests that it should be consistent for the direct effect. Therefore,
\beta_{1},\beta_{0}
should be chosen in order to minimize the asymptotic variance of (
11
). In Example 9 of
[
18
]
, it is shown via properties of least-squares that the optimal choices are the population OLS coefficients in the treatment and control groups separately. Using the plug-in estimates of these OLS coefficients, one recovers Lin’s interacted linear regression. One can also ask the question: what if the coefficients
\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}=:\beta
in (
11
) are equal? Such estimators are natural especially when
n_{0},n-n_{0}
are small relative to
d.
As
[
20
]
shows, the optimal
\beta
can be estimated and the plug-in returns the ToM regression. Because this class of imputation estimators nests the unadjusted estimator and the regression adjustment
Y_{i}\sim Z_{i}+X_{i}
, both Lin’s regression and ToM have better asymptotic variance.

Our results echo these cross-sectional findings and also highlight unexpected differences. Returning to the MRD setting, the analog of ANCOVA (
4
) can hurt asymptotic variance especially in imbalanced experiments, echoing Freedman’s finding. Consider again the imputation estimator in (
7
). We also show that optimal regression adjustment
\beta
is still estimable in this complex setting, even though a priori it depends on unobservable potential outcomes. A further inspection of the first order condition for the optimal
\beta
shows that this should be a generic phenomenon. Using the resulting plug-in gives an interpretable regression in some cases, but not others. For the direct effect, one obtains the weighted TWFE regression in (
6
) whose weights resemble those of ToM. The story is similar for the interacted regression (
10
), revealing optimal regression adjustments that are different than running separate OLS’s in each group and cannot generally be written in a clean regression form.
Table

1
summarizes this discussion and presages some further phenomena discussed throughout the text.

Table 1
:
Parallels and differences between MRDs and classical regression adjustments (A/B).

Phenomenon

MRD

A/B

No modeling assumptions on potential outcomes

✓

✓

ANCOVA can harm asymptotic precision

✓

✓

Optimal adjustments are estimable

✓

✓

Optimal interacted adjustment has regression form

✗

✓ (Lin)

Optimal non-interacted adjustment has regression form

\sim
(Direct Effect)

✓ (ToM)

Optimal interacted and non-interacted adjustments are asymptotically equivalent

✗

✓

Robust standard errors from the corresponding regression directly yield conservative inference

✗

✓

Notes.

\sim
indicates a partial result. For MRDs, the direct effect admits a weighted TWFE regression interpretation, while the total and spillover effects generally do not admit an equally simple regression representation.

1.5
Further Setup & Notation

Before describing our results, let us establish some notation and assumptions. Let
\mathcal{S}=\sigma\{(W_{j}^{\textsf{S}})_{j}\}
denote the sigma algebra generated by the seller treatment indicators, and
\mathcal{B}=\sigma\{(W_{i}^{\textsf{B}})_{i}\}
similarly. Recall
\Gamma:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\}
. We will occasionally use
W^{\textsf{B}},W^{\textsf{S}}
to denote the vector of the treatment indicator variables. For each group
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we will write
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
to denote the buyer indices in group
\gamma,
and similarly with
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
. For example,
\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}=\{i:W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=1\},\mathcal{J}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}=\{j:W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=1\}
. We write
(i,j)\in\gamma
to denote
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma},j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
and occasionally we will use the shorthand
i\in\gamma
and
j\in\gamma
to mean
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma},j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
respectively. Throughout the paper we will use
A\lesssim B
to denote
A\leq CB
for an absolute constant
C
not depending on any asymptotic scaling. Throughout this paper, we will assume that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{I_{T}}{I}\in(0,1),\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{J_{T}}{J}\in(0,1),\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{I}{J}\in(0,\infty).

(12)

Implicitly, each of
I_{T},J_{T},I,J
are indexed by
n\uparrow\infty
which enumerates a sequence of finite populations.

Matrix notation.

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}y_{ij}(\gamma),\quad\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma)=\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{I}\sum_{i=1}^{I}y_{ij}(\gamma),\quad\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma)=\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle y_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=y_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)+\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}.

We will call the operation in the last line
double-decentering
a matrix, forcing row and column means to be zero. Define the following variance quantities for the potential outcomes:

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{I-1}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\bar{y}^{(d)}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)^{2},\ \omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{1}{J-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bar{y}^{(d)}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)^{2},\ \omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}:=\frac{1}{(I-1)(J-1)}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i=1}^{I}y_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma)^{2}.

(13)

\displaystyle\xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{B}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{I-1}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\left(\bar{y}^{(d)}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\bar{y}^{(d)}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma^{\prime})\right)^{2},\ \xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{1}{J-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\bar{y}^{(d)}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)-\bar{y}^{(d)}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma^{\prime})\right)^{2},

(14)

\displaystyle\xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{(I-1)(J-1)}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\left[y_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma)-y_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma^{\prime})\right]^{2}.

(15)

Given a matrix
(A_{ij})_{ij}
, we will use the notation
\omega^{\theta}(A)
for
\theta\in\{\textsf{B},\textsf{S},\textsf{BS}\}
to denote the analogues of the above equations with
A
replacing
y
.

2
Optimal Non-Interacted Regression Adjustments

Returning to regression adjustments for MRDs, we present the general methodology for generic estimands of interest

\tau_{c}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma},

given any vector
c=[c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}},c_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}]
. We consider the non-interacted imputation estimator in (
7
):

\tau_{c}(\beta)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left(y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta\right)

(16)

To describe the form of the optimal regression adjustment
\tilde{\beta}_{c}
that minimizes
\Var(\tau_{c}(\beta)),
we first claim that the variance can be written as a positive definite quadratic form given by

\Var(\tau_{c}(\beta))=\beta^{\top}Z\beta-2u^{\top}\beta+\Var(\tau_{c}),

where
Z
is an appropriate linear combination of
Z^{\textsf{B}},Z^{\textsf{S}},Z^{\textsf{BS}}
and
u
is an appropriate linear combination of
u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}},u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}},u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}
, to be described. By inspecting the variance and covariance formulas in
Propositions

A.1
and
A.2
,
\Var(\tau_{c}(\beta))
can be decomposed as the linear combination of terms
\tilde{\omega}^{\theta}_{\gamma}
for
\theta\in\{\textsf{B},\textsf{S},\textsf{BS}\}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, analogous to those in (
13
), but with residualized outcomes
y_{ij}(\gamma)-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta
instead of
y_{ij}(\gamma)
alongside terms of the form
\xi_{\gamma}^{\theta}
, which do not depend on
\beta
. Define the inner product terms

u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}:=\frac{1}{I-1}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\bar{X}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma),\quad u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}=\frac{1}{J-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bar{X}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma),\quad u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}=\frac{1}{(I-1)(J-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}X_{ij}^{(dcr)}y_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma).

(17)

for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and the gram-matrix terms

Z^{\textsf{B}}:=\frac{1}{I-1}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\bar{X}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}\bar{X}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)\top},\quad Z^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{1}{J-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bar{X}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}\bar{X}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)\top},\quad Z^{\textsf{BS}}:=\frac{1}{(I-1)(J-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}X_{ij}^{(dcr)}X_{ij}^{(dcr)\top},

(18)

Expanding out the square, we find that

\tilde{\omega}^{\theta}_{\gamma}=\beta^{\top}Z^{\theta}\beta-2\beta^{\top}u_{\gamma}^{\theta}+\omega^{\theta}_{\gamma}

for all
\theta\in\{\textsf{B},\textsf{S},\textsf{BS}\}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Summing these terms, we have that
\Var(\tau_{c}(\beta))
is of the form
\beta^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{c}\beta-2\tilde{u}_{c}^{\top}\beta+C
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_{\gamma,\textsf{B}}Z^{\textsf{B}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{S}}Z^{\textsf{S}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{BS}}Z^{\textsf{BS}}

(19)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_{\gamma,\textsf{B}}u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{S}}u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{BS}}u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}

(20)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\Var(\tau_{c})

(21)

The variance formula in
Proposition

A.1
implies that the coefficients satisfy
a_{\gamma,\textsf{B}}=O(1/I),a_{\gamma,\textsf{S}}=O(1/J),a_{\gamma,\textsf{BS}}=O(1/IJ)
. Finally, the quadratic form is positive-definite because its value is nonnegative. The variance-minimizing
\beta
is given by solving the first order condition, since the form is positive-definite. Thus,
\tilde{\beta}_{c}=\tilde{Z}_{c}^{-1}\tilde{u}_{c}
. To estimate the optimal adjustment, we will use

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_{\gamma,\textsf{B}}\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{S}}\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{BS}}\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}

(22)

\displaystyle\hat{u}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}a_{\gamma,\textsf{B}}\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{S}}\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}+a_{\gamma,\textsf{BS}}\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}},

(23)

with the estimators

\begin{split}&\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}:=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\hat{\bar{X}}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma)\hat{\bar{y}}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma),\quad\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}=\frac{1}{J_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}\hat{\bar{X}}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma)\hat{\bar{y}}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma)\\
&\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\hat{X}_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma)\hat{y}_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma).\end{split}

(24)

\begin{split}&\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}:=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\hat{\bar{X}}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma)\hat{\bar{X}}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)\top}(\gamma),\quad\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{1}{J_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}\hat{\bar{X}}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma)\hat{\bar{X}}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)\top}(\gamma)\\
&\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}:=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\hat{X}_{ij}^{(dcr)}(\gamma)\hat{X}_{ij}^{(dcr)\top}(\gamma).\end{split}

(25)

Crucially for each of these estimators, the decentering and double decentering is done within each group
\gamma
. For example,
\hat{\bar{X}}_{i,\bullet}^{(d)}(\gamma):=\frac{1}{J_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}X_{ij}-\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}X_{ij}
. Although the covariates for each unit are known, we use the empirical averages to ensure
\beta
has a projection property detailed in
Lemma

E.4
.

Example 2.1

(Direct Effect)
.

We first derive optimal regression adjustment strategies for the direct effect. As derived in
Section

A.1
, the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\beta)
is given by:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{I_{T}J_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{I_{T}J_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}J_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}J_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\\
&+\frac{I_{C}}{I_{T}I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}-\frac{1}{I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}+\frac{1}{I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}+\frac{1}{I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}-\frac{1}{I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}+\frac{I_{T}}{I_{C}}\frac{1}{I}\xi^{\textsf{B}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}\\
&-\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}+\frac{J_{C}}{J_{T}}\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}+\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}+\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}+\frac{J_{T}}{J_{C}}\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}-\frac{1}{J}\xi^{\textsf{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}\\
&-\frac{I_{C}}{I_{T}}\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}-\frac{J_{C}}{J_{T}}\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}-\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}-\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}-\frac{J_{T}}{J_{C}}\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}-\frac{I_{T}}{I_{C}}\frac{1}{IJ}\xi^{\textsf{BS}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}.\end{split}

(26)

The potential outcome contrasts do not depend on
\beta
, so the objective reduces to minimizing

\frac{1}{I_{T}J_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{I_{T}J_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}J_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}J_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}.

(27)

Solving for the optimal coefficient
\beta
using the previous section, we see that
\tilde{\beta}_{\dir}:=\tilde{Z}_{\dir}^{-1}\tilde{u}_{\dir}
with

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{\dir}:=\sum_{\gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}Z^{\textsf{BS}},\quad\tilde{u}_{\dir}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}u_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}.

Thus, we will use the estimator
\hat{\beta}_{\dir}:=\hat{Z}_{\dir}^{-1}\hat{u}_{\dir}
with

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{\dir}:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\hat{Z}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}},\quad\hat{u}_{\dir}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\hat{u}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}.

There is an interpretation for the regression adjustment in this setting. The resulting plug-in
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\hat{\beta}_{\dir})
turns out to be the coefficient
\tau
in the weighted least squares regression

\displaystyle y_{ij}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{ij}}}{{\sim}}(\mu+\mu^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\mu^{\textsf{S}}_{j})+(\alpha+\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}+(\delta+\delta^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\delta^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+(\tau+\tau^{\textsf{B}}_{i}+\tau^{\textsf{S}}_{j})W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}+X_{ij}^{\top}\beta,

with weights
w_{ij}=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}^{2}J_{\gamma}^{2}}
for
\gamma\ni(i,j).
To make the coefficient
\tau
identifiable, we enforce several constraints among the variables. The appendix gathers the relevant details.

The result is interesting for a few reasons. Firstly, the form of the regression is evocative of cross-sectional ToM, as the groups
\gamma
of smaller size are upweighted in this regression, by a similar factor as in ToM. Moreover, because regression corresponds exactly to an imputation form estimator, our results in
Section

3
show a
no-harm principle
– the estimate is consistent and asymptotically normal around the true direct effect without assuming the linear covariate model. Finally, the weighted TWFE regression asymptotically outperforms no adjustment and the ANCOVA adjustment (
4
), since they are all contained in this class of imputation estimators. Taking
\beta=0
and the least squares
\beta
from the ANCOVA objective in (
7
) yields exactly the latter two procedures. Since the TWFE regression corresponds to the optimal choice of
\beta
, we should always expect an improvement in variance reduction for each of these methods.

Example 2.2

(Total Effect)
.

Conducting the same exercise for the total effect, we see that the variance formula derived in
Section

A.2
simplifies slightly, yielding

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Var(\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}-\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}})&=\frac{1}{I_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{B}}+\frac{1}{J_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{S}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}J_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}}{I_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}J_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}-1\right)\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{BS}}\\
&+\frac{1}{I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}}\omega_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}^{\textsf{B}}+\frac{1}{J_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}}\omega_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}^{\textsf{S}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}J_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}{I_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}J_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}}-1\right)\omega_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}^{\textsf{BS}},\\
\end{split}

(28)

where we again replace the potential outcomes by
y_{ij}(\gamma)-X_{ij}^{\top}\beta
. The resulting expression is a quadratic form in
\beta
, which is positive definite since it is the variance of a random variable. Solving the first order condition gives the optimal
\tilde{\beta}_{\tot}=\tilde{Z}_{\tot}^{-1}\tilde{u}_{\tot}
with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{Z}_{\tot}&:=\left(\frac{1}{I_{T}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{B}}+\left(\frac{1}{J_{T}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{S}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{C}J_{C}}{I_{T}J_{T}}+\frac{I_{T}J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}}-2\right)Z^{\textsf{BS}}\end{split}

(29)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{u}_{\tot}&:=\frac{1}{I_{T}}u^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{J_{T}}u^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{C}J_{C}}{I_{T}J_{T}}-1\right)u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}\\
&+\frac{1}{I_{C}}u^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}u^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{T}J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}}-1\right)u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\end{split}

(30)

As an estimator for
\beta
, we will use

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{Z}_{\tot}&:=\frac{1}{I_{T}}\hat{Z}^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{J_{T}}\hat{Z}^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{C}J_{C}}{I_{T}J_{T}}-1\right)\hat{Z}^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}\\
&+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\hat{Z}^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\hat{Z}^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{T}J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}}-1\right)\hat{Z}^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\\
\hat{u}_{\tot}&:=\frac{1}{I_{T}}\hat{u}^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{J_{T}}\hat{u}^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{C}J_{C}}{I_{T}J_{T}}-1\right)\hat{u}^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}\\
&+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\hat{u}^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\hat{u}^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{1}{IJ}\left(\frac{I_{T}J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}}-1\right)\hat{u}^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}.\end{split}

(31)

In contrast to the direct effect, this appears difficult to write this out as a regression, unless one also includes the row and column means of the matrix as data. This is slightly evocative of Mundlak regression from the panel data literature, where the regression can be decomposed into within-unit and across unit-terms. The optimal MRD adjustments solve a mean-square error objective that can be decomposed into mean-square error terms for the row means, column means, and the doubly decentered matrix. The weights placed on each of these terms makes it difficult to write the adjustment in a clean Mundlak form.

Example 2.3

(Spillover Effects)
.

Computations in
Appendix

A
show that for the buyer spillover, it suffices to choose
\beta
to optimize

\displaystyle\frac{1}{I_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{J_{T}}{I_{T}J_{C}J}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}J}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}},

so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{Z}_{\text{bs}}&:=\left(\frac{1}{I_{T}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{B}}+\frac{J_{T}}{J_{C}J}\left(\frac{1}{I_{T}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{BS}}\\
\tilde{u}_{\text{bs}}&:=\frac{1}{I_{T}}u^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{1}{I_{C}}u^{\textsf{B}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{J_{T}}{I_{T}J_{C}J}u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}+\frac{J_{T}}{I_{C}J_{C}J}u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}.\end{split}

(32)

For the seller spillover effect,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{J_{T}}\omega^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\omega^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{I_{T}}{J_{T}I_{C}I}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{I_{T}}{J_{C}I_{C}I}\omega^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}.

The optimal adjustments are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{Z}_{\text{ss}}&:=\left(\frac{1}{J_{T}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{S}}+\frac{I_{T}}{I_{C}I}\left(\frac{1}{J_{T}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}\right)Z^{\textsf{BS}}\\
\tilde{u}_{\text{ss}}&:=\frac{1}{J_{T}}u^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{1}{J_{C}}u^{\textsf{S}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}+\frac{I_{T}}{J_{T}I_{C}I}u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\frac{I_{T}}{J_{C}I_{C}I}u^{\textsf{BS}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}.\end{split}

(33)

Regression-adjusted estimators may be derived in a similar fashion to the total and direct effects; these adjustments do not seem to have particularly nice interpretations as weighted least squares.

3
Asymptotic Theory

To state our asymptotic guarantees on the regression adjustment procedure, we apply an improved CLT for MRDs discussed in
Section

3.2
to obtain a central limit theorem for the oracle
\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c})
. We then show that the plug-in
\hat{\tau}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})
has the same asymptotic distribution as the oracle estimator and derive inference procedures. Throughout this section, fix a coefficient vector
c:=(c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},c_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},c_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}})
and the associated adjustments
\tilde{\beta}_{c},\hat{\beta}_{c}
from
Section

2
. All proofs can be found in
Appendix

E
. The main assumptions are stated below. Let
e_{ij}(\gamma):=y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\tilde{\beta}_{c}.

Assumption 1

(Finite Population Limit)
.

Assume the quantities
\omega^{\theta}_{\gamma},u_{\gamma}^{\theta},Z^{\theta}
have limits for
\theta\in\{\textsf{B},\textsf{S},\textsf{BS}\}
and
\gamma\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\}
.

Assumption 2

(Small Residuals)
.

We suppose that

\begin{split}\max\bigg(&\frac{1}{I}\max_{i=1}^{I}|\bar{e}^{(d)}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)|,\frac{1}{J}\max_{j=1}^{J}|\bar{e}_{\bullet,j}^{(d)}(\gamma)|,\\
&\frac{\sqrt{\log I}}{I^{3/2}}\max_{ij}|e_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{e}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)|,\frac{\sqrt{\log J}}{J^{3/2}}\max_{ij}|e_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{e}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)|,\\
&\frac{1}{I^{2}}\norm{e_{ij}(\gamma) - \dbar{e}_\gamma}_{\text{op}}\bigg)=o(\Var(\widehat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c}))^{1/2}).\end{split}

(34)

Assumption 3

(Covariate Control)
.

For each coordinate
k=1,\dots,d
,

\frac{1}{I}\max_{i,j}|X_{ij}^{(k)}-\bar{\bar{X}}^{(k)}|^{2}=o(1).

(35)

Assumption 4

(Non-vanishing Buyer Seller Variation)
.

We will assume the variance lower bound

\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c}))=\Omega(I^{-1})

(36)

and
I\sum_{\gamma}a_{\gamma,\theta}Z^{\theta}
for
\theta\in\{\textsf{B},\textsf{S}\}
converges to an invertible matrix.

Papers in the finite population literature
[
19
,
18
,
17
,
20
]
often require similar conditions as
1
,
2
and
3
.
4
does not have direct precedent in the literature on cross-sectional adjustments. The new challenge for multiple randomization designs, as mentioned previously, is the possibility of multiple scaling regimes for the variance as well as
\tilde{Z}_{c},\tilde{u}_{c}
. For example, it is possible that
Z^{\textsf{B}},Z^{\textsf{S}}\rightarrow 0
while
u^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma},u^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}
do not, so that
\tilde{\beta}_{c}
blows up.
4
is a convenient starting point for our analysis. The first condition (
36
) is the primary assumption in
[
23
]
and the second condition enforces
\tilde{\beta}_{c}=O(1)
. We show later in this section how to go beyond this assumption in an important example.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose
1
,
2
and
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}=0
. Then

\frac{\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c})-\tau_{c}}{\sqrt{\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c}))}}\Rightarrow\textsf{N}(0,1).

This follows as an immediate consequence of
Corollary

3.1
and the balancing condition on
c
. As discussed in the introduction,

Theorem

3.1
shows a form of model robustness: we do not need to assume that
y_{ij}(\gamma)
follows a linear model in the covariates. These results are true of Lin’s estimator and the ToM regression
[
19
,
20
]
.
[
6
]
develops another approach to these so-called no-harm principles using calibration for nonlinear models.

Proposition 3.1
.

Under
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, the estimator
\hat{\beta}_{c}
in
Section

2
is consistent for the optimal coefficient
\tilde{\beta}_{c}
. That is,
\hat{\beta}_{c}-\tilde{\beta}_{c}=o_{p}(1).

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
and
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}=0
. Then the oracle adjusted estimator
\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c})
and the regression adjusted estimator
\hat{\tau}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})
have the same asymptotic distribution.

3.1
Conservative Variance Estimation and Inference

In order to conduct inference on an effect
\hat{\tau}_{c}
of interest, it suffices to consider conservative variance estimators for
\Var(\hat{\tau}_{c}(\tilde{\beta}_{c}))
. The most straightforward idea is to take the residuals
\hat{e}_{ij}:=y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{c}
and plug them into the conservative variance estimator of
[
23
]
. Section 4.4 of
[
23
]
supplies said conservative variance estimator
\hat{V}_{c}
. To review the construction, we define

\hat{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}:=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\left[\hat{\bar{y}}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma}\right]^{2},\quad\hat{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{1}{J_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}}\left[\hat{\bar{y}}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma}\right]^{2}

(37)

and the estimator counterpart for
\omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}
by

\hat{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}:=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left[y_{ij}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{y}}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{y}}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)+\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma}\right]^{2}.

(38)

Let

\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{I-I_{\gamma}}{II_{\gamma}},\quad\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{J-J_{\gamma}}{JJ_{\gamma}}.

(39)

Then Theorem 4.4 of
[
23
]
demonstrates that an unbiased estimator for
\Var(\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma})
is given by

\begin{split}\hat{\Sigma}_{\gamma}&:=\frac{\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}+\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}+\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\textsf{BS}}_{\gamma}}{1-\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}-\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}+\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}}\\
&-\frac{\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}}{1-\alpha^{\textsf{B}}_{\gamma}}\frac{J-J_{\gamma}}{J(J_{\gamma}-1)}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left(y_{ij}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{y}}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)\right)^{2}\\
&-\frac{\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}}{1-\alpha^{\textsf{S}}_{\gamma}}\frac{I-I_{\gamma}}{I(I_{\gamma}-1)}\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\gamma}\left(y_{ij}(\gamma)-\hat{\bar{y}}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(40)

As a result, one can estimate the conservative upper bound

V_{c}:=\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|c_{\gamma}|\sqrt{\Var(\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma})}\right)^{2}

(41)

using the conservative variance estimator

\hat{V}_{c}=\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|c_{\gamma}|\sqrt{\hat{\Sigma}_{\gamma}^{+}}\right)^{2}.

(42)

where
x^{+}=\max(x,0)
.

Plugging in the residuals
\hat{e}_{ij}:=y_{ij}-X_{ij}^{\top}\hat{\beta}_{c}
into (
42
), yields the plug-in estimate
\hat{V}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})
. Using this we can create an asymptotically conservative confidence interval given by

\hat{\tau}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\hat{V}_{c}(\hat{\beta}_{c})^{1/2}.

(43)

The next theorem gives assumptions under which the confidence interval is valid, leveraging a similar improvement in conservative variance estimation for MRDs detailed in the next subsection.

Theorem 3.2

(Valid Conservative Inference)
.

Under
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, the conservative confidence interval in (
43
) has asymptotic coverage at least
1-\alpha
.

In some cases, we can also go beyond
4
. An important example is inference for the direct effect.

Example 3.1

(Inference for the Direct Effect)
.

If one inspects the case of the direct effect in
Example

2.1
, the regression adjustment minimizes (
27
). But notice that this term is of order
O((IJ)^{-1})
, suggesting that direct effect adjustment is meaningful asymptotically only when buyer and seller mean variation is of lower order. Our tools can theoretically show regression adjustment works in this case. To illustrate, suppose that

\omega^{\textsf{B}}(y_{ij}(\gamma))=O(1/I),\quad\omega^{\textsf{S}}(y_{ij}(\gamma))=O(1/I)

for each
\gamma
and similarly for each coordinate of the covariates. To avoid degenerate cases, suppose further that
\omega^{\textsf{BS}}(e_{ij}(\gamma))\not\rightarrow 0
and
\lim Z^{\textsf{BS}}
is invertible. In particular, this implies that the variance of the direct effect estimator is
\Theta(1/IJ)
.

Suppose
1
,
2
and
3
and the stronger covariate regularity condition

\max_{i}|\bar{X}_{i,\bullet}-\bar{\bar{X}}|,\quad\max_{j}|\bar{X}_{\bullet,j}-\bar{\bar{X}}|=O(1),

(44)

for each coordinate
k.
Under these assumptions, one can establish the conclusions of
Propositions

3.2
and
3.2
. The idea is to apply
Theorem

3.1
to
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\tilde{\beta}_{\dir})
and note that
\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\hat{\beta}_{\dir})-\hat{\tau}_{\dir}(\tilde{\beta}_{\dir})
is given by

\left(\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}-\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}-\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}\right)^{\top}(\hat{\beta}_{\dir}-\tilde{\beta}_{\dir}).

The covariate difference term
\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}-\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}-\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}+\hat{\bar{\bar{X}}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}
is small: using the variance formula for the direct effect in
Section

A.1
, the variance of each coordinate is of order
O(1/IJ).
Exploiting this gives the results. Details can be found in the appendix.

3.2
Improved MRD Theory

Our results on the plug-in optimal adjustment utilize improvements to the theory of MRDs, strengthening the conclusions and relaxing some assumptions of
[
23
]
. The first improvement is a quantitative CLT for the doubly randomized sum
\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}.
The approach uses finite CLTs and tensorization properties in the
1
-Wasserstein distance and somewhat simplifies the argument in
[
23
]
by avoiding multivariate quantitative CLTs. Although the bounds are not directly comparable since the CLT of
[
23
]
uses Kolmogorov distance, our new result shows asymptotic normality
without two previous assumptions: 1) boundedness and 2) variance lower bound
\Var(\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}})=\Omega(I^{-1}+J^{-1})
. The proof and examples are given in
Appendix

C
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Consider an
I\times J
matrix
Y
with entries
y_{ij}
. Let
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
be indicator variables for sampling
I_{T}
rows and
J_{T}
columns respectively. Let
\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}=\frac{1}{I_{T}J_{T}}\sum_{i,j}W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}y_{ij}
,
\bar{\bar{y}}
be the grand mean
\frac{1}{IJ}\sum_{i,j}y_{ij}
, and define
\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}^{2}:=\Var(\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}})
. We have the bound

\displaystyle d_{W}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}-\bar{\bar{y}}}{\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}}\right),\textsf{N}\left(0,1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{I}}+\frac{1}{I}\frac{\max_{i=1}^{I}\left|\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}-\bar{\bar{y}}\right|}{\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}}+\frac{1}{J}\frac{\max_{j=1}^{J}\left|\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}-\bar{\bar{y}}\right|}{\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}}

(45)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sqrt{\log I}}{I^{3/2}}\frac{\max_{i,j}|y_{ij}-\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}|}{\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}}+\left(\frac{1}{I^{2}}\frac{\norm{y_{ij} - \dbar{y}}_{\text{op}}}{\sigma_{\textsf{Tot}}}\right)^{1/2}.

(46)

Roughly, the theorem is proved by conditioning on the seller variables
\mathcal{S}
and applying a finite population central limit theorem in the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein distance
[
11
]
. The sketch of course suggests that we may condition first on the buyer variables
B
, obtaining an analogous result to
Theorem

3.3
where the right-hand-side applies to
Y^{\top}
. This can yield better guarantees, especially when the matrix is imbalanced.
Theorem

3.3
can be used to establish a CLT for various MRD estimators.

Corollary 3.1
.

Consider any MRD estimator
\widehat{\tau}_{c}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\hat{\bar{\bar{y}}}_{\gamma},

and suppose that for each set of potential outcomes
(y_{ij}(\gamma))_{ij},\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have the analogue of Eq. (
64
):

\begin{split}&\max\bigg(\frac{1}{I}\max_{i=1}^{I}|\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}|,\frac{1}{J}\max_{j=1}^{J}|\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}|,\\
&\max_{i,j}\frac{\sqrt{\log I}}{I^{3/2}}|y_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)|,\frac{1}{I^{2}}\norm{y_{ij}(\gamma) - \dbar{y}_\gamma}_{\text{op}}\bigg)=o(\Var(\widehat{\tau}_{c})^{1/2}).\end{split}

(47)

Then

\frac{\widehat{\tau}_{c}-\tau_{c}}{\Var(\widehat{\tau}_{c})^{1/2}}\Rightarrow\textsf{N}(0,1).

In the appendix, we show
Theorem

3.3
recovers the central limit theorem of
[
23
]
with bounded outcomes and a lower bound on the variance.
Examples

C.1
,
C.2
and
C.3
in the appendix give several examples where this
Corollary

3.1
may apply but the CLT of
[
23
]
does not. The idea behind these examples is vanishing variation in the row and column means, which often appears due to
sparsity
. It is often the case in real two-sided marketplaces that only a small fraction of the
I\times J
potential interactions between buyers and sellers actually take place.
Theorem

3.3
can establish asymptotic normality in these settings.

Our next result shows that the conservative variance estimator is consistent in Theorem 4.4 of
[
23
]
, again relaxing the need for a
\Theta(I^{-1})
lower bound on the variance. The assumption can be slightly relaxed; in the third assumption of
Corollary

3.1
, we only need
I^{-3/2}\max_{i,j}|y_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)|=o(\Var(\widehat{\tau}_{c})^{1/2}).

Theorem 3.4

(Consistent Variance Estimation)
.

Suppose (
47
) with the additional condition
\frac{\sqrt{\log I}}{I^{3/2}}|y_{ij}(\gamma)-\bar{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)|=o(\Var(\widehat{\tau}_{c})^{1/2}).
Then

\frac{\hat{V}_{c}}{V_{c}}\xrightarrow{p}1.

4
Simulation Results

We present two simulation settings comparing three methods for various target estimands: the unadjusted MRD estimator, ANCOVA, and the plug-in optimal non-interacted estimator. For simplicity, we will refer to the latter estimator as Opt. We plot sampling distributions of each estimator along with averaged coverage and lengths of confidence intervals obtained from the plug-in procedure. The simulations randomize the assignment variables
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
over 5000 Monte Carlo runs. Overall, we corroborate the
no-harm
property of the optimal adjustment – in large samples, the method is guaranteed to be at least as efficient as the other methods. In some settings, the improvement can be substantial. Throughout this section, we will roughly refer to an experiment as
imbalanced
if the ratios
I_{T}/I,J_{T}/J
are small and
balanced
if
I_{T}/I\approx J_{T}/J\approx 0.5
.

We first consider a toy synthetic example, which is also the setting of
Figure

1
in the introduction. The example considers
Y_{ij}(\gamma)\sim\textsf{N}(\mu_{\gamma},1)
from
Example

C.1
. We take
(\mu_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}},\mu_{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},\mu_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},\mu_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}})=(5,2,2,1)
and three specifications for the number of treated buyers and sellers, where
\{10\%,20\%,50\%\}
of the buyers and sellers are treated. In this semisynthetic simulation, we provide a four-dimensional covariate where the coordinates are noisy versions of
Y_{ij}(\gamma),\gamma\in\Gamma
and the target is the direct effect estimand. Although unrealistic, this example showcases the setting of
Example

3.1
where the theory of
[
23
]
does not apply. The results are displayed in
Figures

2
and
3
.

Opt outperforms both methods in all cases, delivering significant gains in efficiency for imbalanced experiments. For a balanced experiment, Opt and ANCOVA perform very similarly. For imbalanced experiments, we also find that the ANCOVA procedure has
larger variance
than the unadjusted procedure, extending the findings of
[
10
,
9
]
where the naive regression adjustment might worsen asymptotic precision in cross-sectional experiments. All methods overcover the target, with Opt continuing to deliver the shortest confidence intervals.

Figure 2
:
Sampling distributions for Unadjusted, ANCOVA, and Optimal Non-Interacted adjustments over 5000 Monte Carlo samples, on a fixed realization of the i.i.d. normal data generating process of
Example

C.1
with parameters described in the text. The direct effect is the estimand. Monte Carlo randomizes over
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
. The three panels correspond to three scenarios for ratio
I_{T}/I=J_{T}/J
,
\{0.1,0.2,0.5\}.
Legends show estimated standard deviations of each method.

Figure 3
:
Coverage and CI length for Unadjusted, ANCOVA, and Optimal Non-Interacted adjustments averaged over 5000 Monte Carlo samples, on a fixed realization of the i.i.d. normal data generating process of
Example

C.1
with parameters described in the text. The direct effect is the estimand. Parameters are described in the text. Monte Carlo randomizes over
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
. The three panels correspond to three scenarios for ratio
I_{T}/I=J_{T}/J
,
\{0.1,0.2,0.5\}.

In the second set of simulations, we instantiate a more realistic synthetic model taken from Example 2.5 of
[
23
]
. The example models a marketplace of content creators and advertisers, indexed by
i,j
respectively. Each creator
i
produces content with
score

q_{i}^{c}
and each advertiser places ads with corresponding quality
q_{j}^{a}
. Each creator-advertiser pair generates revenue
y_{ij}
equal to
m_{ij}(q_{i}^{c}+q_{j}^{a})
for some notion of compatibility
m_{ij}
. For some pre-negotiated parameters
r_{i}^{c},r_{j}^{a}
, creators and advertisers are compensated
r_{i}^{c}y_{ij},r_{j}^{a}y_{ij}
respectively with the rest going to the platform. A binary intervention
\mathbf{w}
is tested which allocates a subsidy
\eta
, affecting revenue as follows:

y_{ij}(\mathbf{w})=(m_{ij}+\eta w_{ij})\{q_{i}^{c}(\mathbf{w})+q_{j}^{a}(\mathbf{w})\}

A simple model of incentives for both creators and advertisers leads to a Nash equilibrium where the revenue
y_{ij}(\mathbf{w})
satisfies the local interference condition (see Example 2.5 of
[
23
]
). We consider nearly the same parameters as in Section 5 of that work, with
\eta=5
, i.i.d. parameters
m_{ij}\sim\text{Exp}(1)
, and
r_{i}^{c},r_{j}^{a}\sim\text{Unif}[0,0.2]
across advertisers

i\in[I],j\in[J]
. We take
I=200,J=150
and consider the covariate vector
X_{ij}=(\hat{m}_{ij}r_{i}^{c},\hat{m}_{ij}r_{j}^{a})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
We assume the planner has access to a noisy observation of the compatibility
m
, given by
\hat{m}_{ij}\sim m_{ij}(1+\textsf{N}(0,0.1))
. Three specifications are considered:
(I_{T},J_{T})=\{(20,15),(40,30),(100,75)\}.
We will test our methods for the buyer spillover effect in this example. The results are displayed in
Figures

4
and
5
.

Figure 4
:
Sampling distributions for Unadjusted, ANCOVA, and Optimal Non-Interacted adjustments over 5000 Monte Carlo samples, on a fixed realization of the marketplace data generating process of
[
23
]
. The buyer spillover effect is the estimand. Parameters are described in the text.

Figure 5
:
Coverage and CI length for Unadjusted, ANCOVA, and Optimal Non-Interacted adjustments averaged over 5000 Monte Carlo samples, on a fixed realization of the marketplace data generating process of
[
23
]
. The buyer spillover effect is the estimand. Parameters are described in the text.

Both methods for regression adjustment significantly outperform the unadjusted method in all metrics. ANCOVA and Opt perform similarly in imbalanced experiments, with Opt being slightly more efficient. Opt significantly outperforms ANCOVA for balanced experiments in efficiency. However, the reduction in confidence interval length is not as large as the reduction in variance. It may be fruitful to consider optimal regression adjustments which optimize
\beta
to minimize length of the conservative confidence interval, but this is at odds with the natural objective of finding a minimum variance estimator.

Inspecting the two simulation settings, Opt significantly outperforms ANCOVA for different ratios of treatment and control, in different data generating processes. Although a theoretical analysis could be done to analyze when this occurs, the no-harm principle seems robust enough to practically recommend Opt over ANCOVA. In practice, we also recommend some attention to the choice of covariates. Notice that the direct effect estimator (
7
) cannot use covariates which are constant across either buyers or sellers; similarly, the imputation estimator for the buyer and seller spillovers cannot take advantage of covariates which are the same across sellers and buyers, respectively.

5
Discussion

In this work, we propose optimal regression adjustments for estimands in multiple randomization designs. These adjustments are model-robust and satisfy no-harm principles which guarantee improvement over other baselines such as ANCOVA or unadjusted MRD estimators. We establish central limit theorems for these optimal regression adjustments as well as methods for conservative inference. These results build on improved tools that also strengthen the results in
[
23
]
. Our tools can also be used to analyze optimal interacted regression adjustments; we provide formulas and numerical results for these interacted adjustments in
Appendix

F
, but leave detailed theoretical analysis for future work.

5.1
Directions for Future Research

Further Questions on MRDs.

As discussed in the introduction, a full investigation of the scaling limits of MRDs would constitute a substantial body of future research. Investigating all reasonable asymptotic limits for doubly randomized sums, like in
Theorem

3.3
, would be a first step. For example, it is possible to have non-normal limits. Let
Y_{ij}
be an
I\times I
square matrix and equal to a rank one matrix
xx^{\top}
for some vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{I}
. The doubly randomized sum can be factored into

\hat{\bar{\bar{Y}}}=\left(\frac{1}{I_{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}x_{i}\right)\left(\frac{1}{J_{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}x_{j}\right).

When
\bar{x}=0,
one can show that the last term in the bound of
Theorem

3.3
fails to vanish and
\hat{\bar{\bar{Y}}}
converges weakly to the product of two independent normals. The situation bears some resemblance to the theory of second-order
U
-statistics. Characterizing all possible distributional limits and developing methods for uniform inference would be an interesting direction to explore. In a related but different setting,
[
3
]
characterizes the possible distributional limits of
quadratic chaos
: quantities of the form
\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}X_{i}X_{j}
where
a_{ij}
is a symmetric
\{0,1\}
-valued symmetric matrix and
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are iid random variables with zero mean and variance
1
. It is shown that possible limits are independent sums of a Gaussian, a weighted sum of independent chi-squared random variables, and a Gaussian mixture with random variance. It is possible a similar decomposition holds for doubly randomized sums.

Parallels and Differences with the Cross-Sectional Case.

As we emphasize throughout the paper, several of our findings echo results in the cross-sectional case. However, several questions remain.
[
19
]
shows that the Huber-White standard errors in the cross-sectional interacted linear regression serve as conservative variance estimators suitable for inference. Is it possible to extend this result to the interacted TWFE for the direct effect? Secondly,
[
19
]
shows that the tyranny-of-minority and interacted regressions are both asymptotically the same. Preliminary investigation suggests that both analogues are false in the MRD setting for the direct effect, but further work would be interesting. Separately,
[
17
]
explore regression adjustments in a high-dimensional setting using debiasing techniques, which one can extend to the MRD setting. Nonlinear regression adjustment strategies are also important here. Some outcomes in multiple randomization designs are naturally modelled as Poisson or logistic regressions. Extending the results of
[
12
]
and
[
6
]
would be valuable.

Optimized Conservative Variance Bounds.

It would be interesting to find variance estimators which are less conservative than those proposed in
Section

3.2
. For example, analyzing the direct effect formula in the appendix suggests alternative ways to derive conservative variance bounds, using Cauchy-Schwarz and throwing away negative covariance terms.
[
13
]
provides an optimization approach to finding least-conservative variance estimators in cross-sectional experiments. Pursuing similar ideas could yield sharper inferences in multiple randomization designs.

Appendix A
Variance and Covariance Formulas for MRD Estimators

This section records the results of
[
23
]
, with some inconsequential differences in definitions, for example switching
1/I
with
1/(I-1)
. As we work under an asymptotic framework, we will interchange
1/I
and
1/(I-1)
. Firstly, we define the demeaned treatment indicators

D_{i}^{\textsf{B}}=W_{i}^{\textsf{B}}-\frac{I_{T}}{I},\text{ and }D_{j}^{\textsf{S}}=W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}-\frac{J_{T}}{J}.

(48)

Lemma A.3 of
[
23
]
shows that
D_{i}^{\textsf{B}},D_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
are mean zero and characterizes their variance and covariance. Recall the notation

\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}:=\frac{1}{IJ}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}y_{ij}(\gamma)

Further, define the following average-like quantities:

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}y_{ij}(\gamma),\ \overline{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma):=\frac{1}{I}\sum_{i=1}^{I}y_{ij}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\overline{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma},\ \delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}(\gamma):=\overline{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)-\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=y_{ij}(\gamma)-\overline{y}_{i,\bullet}(\gamma)-\overline{y}_{\bullet,j}(\gamma)+\bar{\bar{y}}_{\gamma}

and the following variance-like quantities:

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I}[\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}}(\gamma)]^{2}}{I-1},\ \omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{J}[\delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}(\gamma)]^{2}}{J-1}

(49)

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}[\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}(\gamma)]^{2}}{(I-1)(J-1)}

(50)

\displaystyle\xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{B}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I}[\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}}(\gamma)-\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}}(\gamma^{\prime})]^{2}}{I-1},\ \xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{S}}:=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{J}[\delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}(\gamma)-\delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}(\gamma^{\prime})]^{2}}{J-1}

(51)

\displaystyle\xi_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I}\sum_{j=1}^{J}[\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}(\gamma)-\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}(\gamma^{\prime})]^{2}}{(I-1)(J-1)}.

(52)

Notice that the
\xi
quantities are all zero when
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}.
Next, we define the average residuals for each
\gamma\in\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}},{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}\}:

\displaystyle\overline{\epsilon}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{B}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}D_{i}^{\textsf{B}}\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}}(\gamma)

(53)

\displaystyle\overline{\epsilon}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{S}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J_{\gamma}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}D_{j}^{\textsf{S}}\delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}(\gamma)

(54)

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\epsilon}}_{\gamma}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{I_{\gamma}J_{\gamma}}\sum_{i,j}D_{i}^{\textsf{B}}D_{j}^{\textsf{S}}\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}(\gamma).

(55)

Because
\delta_{i}^{\textsf{B}},\delta_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
sum to zero and
\delta_{ij}^{\textsf{BS}}
has zero row and column sums, the quantities remain unchanged if we replace
D_{i}^{\textsf{B}},D_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
by
W_{i}^{\textsf{B}},W_{j}^{\textsf{S}}
. The groupwise average estimators can be decomposed in terms of the average residuals, as the next lemma shows.

Lemma A.1

(Hoeffding Decomposition of Groupwise Averages, Lemma A.4 of
[
23
]
)
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{y}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}+\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{B}}+\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{S}}+\bar{\bar{\epsilon}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{tr}}}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{y}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}+\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}^{\textsf{B}}-\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}^{\textsf{S}}-\bar{\bar{\epsilon}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\operatorname{ib}}}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bar{y}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}-\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}^{\textsf{B}}+\overline{\epsilon}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}^{\textsf{S}}-\bar{\bar{\epsilon}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{is}}}^{\textsf{BS}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\overline{\overline{Y}}}_{\operatorname{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cc}}}}
